Best option: 'coverage-guaranteed abstention for markerless clinical kinematics under population shift'. Calibrate metric-level conformal intervals on healthy participants and test whether coverage holds on impaired participants. Use honesty or occlusion labels as conditioning features (Mondrian or covariate-weighted conformal), and use the measured object as a self-supervised anchor that provides a per-trial calibration signal (wrist to object-implied grip point) at test time, with no reference system needed. That anchor is the one ingredient no cited work has.
Validate against a borrowed optical mocap session rather than IMUs or palpation, and release the dataset.
Alternative: drop the instrument framing and publish only the object-clocked event-timing result (grasp and release timing error in ms vs mocap and video, compared with hand-only methods) as a short TNSRE or EMBC paper.
Treat the Unity twin and the functional calibration as separate follow-ups, not as co-equal contributions.
Before committing, run a real 2024-2026 literature search (Cotton lab confidence-interval markerless papers, Azure Kinect drinking-task validation, automated BBT/ARAT 2023-2026). This review could not perform one.

### feasibility skeptic: score 3.5, refuted=True

Verdict: not feasible as scoped for the assumed lab. I default to refuted. Note that the web-search budget was exhausted (200 of 200), so no 2024-2026 novelty check could be run, and every citation is marked unverified.

1. Four projects bundled as one. Object-clocked phase segmentation, conformal metric intervals with abstention, functional segment calibration, and a Unity sensor-noise twin with a learned placement surrogate are each a paper-sized effort with its own validation needs. A 12-18 month student with one D435 cannot deliver all four to a standard that TNSRE or JNER would accept.

2. Ground truth is the fatal gap. Conformal coverage is only as good as the reference it is calibrated against, and the lab has no mocap.
- An IMU suit gives segment orientations, so it can serve as a reference for elbow angle and trunk flexion angle. It cannot give reach extent in cm, joint-centre position in mm, trunk displacement in cm or workspace volume without drift and its own anatomical calibration.
- Two-iPhone OpenCap is itself a keypoint-based estimate with about 4-6 deg error, so calibrating against it certifies agreement, not accuracy.
- Palpation and tape give static landmarks only.
- The partner-lab mocap session, the clinical partner and the trained assessor are named as needs, with no plan for securing them.
- The functional-calibration result (joint-centre mm error, change in metric bias) is not measurable without marker-based reference. Needham et al. needed mocap for exactly this claim.

3. People and ethics. 30-40 healthy participants with repeat sessions, plus 10-15 patients through a hospital partner, means two ethics protocols and recruitment the lab has never done. The thesis has one subject.
- Exchangeable conformal calibration across subjects needs leave-subject-out splits. With about 30 subjects, per-metric coverage at 90 percent will have wide uncertainty.
- Without patients, the clinical-instrument claim stays motivational. Scripted impairments in the twin are not pathology.

4. Unsolved upstream weaknesses.
- Trunk displacement depends on the hip-occlusion and torso-frame failure. The thesis lists it as unsolved, and co-shifting hips fool every detector.
- Elbow extension depends on depth along the optical axis, which the thesis showed is fragile.
- SPARC computed from 30 fps landmark jitter is filter-sensitive.
- So several consensus metrics may abstain most of the time. That is honest, but it makes for a weak paper.

5. The twin sub-project is engineering-dominated. It needs a D435 noise renderer, retargeting, parametric impairments, and 6-10 real configurations with reference error to compute a Spearman correlation. Every real configuration again needs the reference system the lab lacks.

6. Venue mismatch with the user goal. The realistic outlets are TNSRE, JNER and J Biomech, not CVPR or NeurIPS. Single-RGB-D upper-limb assessment is a crowded area (many Kinect-based BBT and ARAT systems), so novelty depends on the object-clock and abstention angle.

What is feasible is the object-anchored part. The marker branch is already precise: rail median 0.26 cm, and the thesis characterized it. Object-derived events and object-path metrics can be validated with cheap, objective references. Elbow and trunk angles can be validated with three IMUs, a reference that is accepted for joint angles.

Minimum viable experiment (3-5 months, minimal-risk ethics):
- Participants: 10-12 healthy adults.
- Task: a seated tagged-cube transfer between two pads fitted with force-sensitive resistors read by an Arduino and hardware-synced to the D435 by an LED flash in view. The FSRs give lift-off and placement ground truth to about 5 ms.
- References: three IMUs (sternum, upper arm, forearm) for elbow-extension and trunk-flexion angles, plus hand-labelled video for grasp onset.
- Show that:
  - object-clocked segmentation times lift-off and placement to within about 1-2 frames of the FSR truth, and grasp onset more accurately than a hand-only MediaPipe or RTMPose velocity baseline;
  - movement time, peak object speed and object-path SPARC agree with FSR- and IMU-derived values (Bland-Altman, ICC);
  - split-conformal intervals for elbow extension and trunk flexion, calibrated leave-subject-out against the IMU reference, reach about 90 percent coverage;
  - honesty-gated abstention lowers error on the trials that are reported, against a no-gating ablation.

If coverage holds and gating measurably helps, the core claim is shown. Functional calibration, the twin and the patient pilot then become separate follow-ups, each conditional on obtaining a mocap session.

- phase events and object-path metrics taken from the fiducial-tagged object, not the occluded hand;
- per-trial conformal intervals for the two joint metrics the D435 chain can plausibly measure (elbow extension and trunk flexion angle);
- abstention driven by the thesis honesty labels.

Validate against references the lab can actually get: FSR contact pads for event truth, three IMUs for the joint angles, and hand-labelled video. Use 10-12 healthy adults and leave-subject-out calibration. Baselines are hand-only keypoint velocity segmentation, a no-gating ablation and Gaussian-only intervals.

Target TNSRE, JNER or IEEE JBHI. Treat functional segment calibration (it needs a borrowed mocap session), the Unity noise twin, and the stroke or PD pilot (it needs a clinical partner and ethics) as later, separately scoped papers. Do not promise them in the first 12 months. Be explicit that this is a rehabilitation-engineering venue track, not CVPR, NeurIPS or CoRL.

### venue skeptic: score 3.5, refuted=True

Area-chair view, target TNSRE / JNER (the stated best fit). No literature search could be run (the session's web search budget was exhausted), so every prior-work entry comes from memory and is unverified. (1) The pitch has no single headline claim. It bundles four papers: object-clocked phase segmentation, conformal metric intervals with abstention, functional segment calibration, and a Unity sensor twin for placement design. Each gets thin validation, and a reviewer will say it is unfocused. (2) Novelty is incremental for the target venues. Single depth-camera upper-limb assessment (Kinect and Azure Kinect reach kinematics, automated Box and Block, Fugl-Meyer automation) has appeared in TNSRE, JNER and Sensors for a decade. Using a fiducial-tagged object as an event clock is an engineering convenience, not a research contribution. It also breaks normed standardized tests, which clinical reviewers will flag at once. The one component with real methodological novelty for this community is per-trial calibrated intervals with abstention, and it is buried. (3) The evaluation is the decisive weakness. An IMU-suit or palpation/tape reference is not accepted ground truth for joint-centre or trunk metrics at TNSRE/JNER/J Biomech. Optical mocap on the full cohort is the expected reference, and the author has none. Conformal coverage computed against a drifting IMU reference certifies agreement with the IMU, not with the truth. Healthy-only cohorts make the clinical claim motivational. The patient pilot (10-15 people via a partner not yet secured) is the part that would make it publishable, and it is 6-12 months out. (4) Several baselines are missing or weak: Azure Kinect body tracking, multi-view markerless systems (Theia, Pose2Sim, OpenCap with a proper upper-limb evaluation), and IMU-based clinical systems. Comparing only against MediaPipe+depth without the object and a stopwatch looks like a strawman. (5) Known thesis weaknesses carry over directly. Trunk displacement relies on hips the desk hides, twist of the hanging arm is unobservable, and the forearm twist froze in a way that put the wrists 12 cm apart. SPARC and peak speed are noise-sensitive under D435 depth noise, so several consensus metrics may abstain most of the time, which weakens the headline. (6) Relative to the user's goal of top venues: TNSRE/JNER are the right tier for this work, but at best it would be a solid validation paper rather than a field-shaping one. As pitched, its expected acceptance at TNSRE is modest. The digital-twin placement study belongs at a smaller venue (EMBC, Sensors) unless a real sim-to-real correlation is shown. Default to refute because the contribution is diffuse, the reference standard is weak, and the clinical cohort is missing.

Prior work found:
- Standardized measurement of quality of upper limb movement after stroke: consensus-based core recommendations from the Second Stroke Recovery and Rehabilitation Roundtable (Neurorehabilitation and Neural Repair 2019); overlap: Defines the metric set and reference tasks (drinking task, reach-to-grasp) the idea proposes to measure; a TNSRE/JNER reviewer will expect the protocol to follow it, which conflicts with a custom tagged-cube transfer task.; verified=False
- OpenCap: Human movement dynamics from smartphone videos (PLOS Computational Biology 2023); overlap: Low-cost markerless kinematics validated against mocap; the obvious baseline. Its point estimates without per-trial uncertainty are the gap the idea claims, but OpenCap is lower-limb focused, so the comparison may look like a strawman unless upper-limb validation is done.; verified=False
- StrokeRehab: A benchmark dataset for sub-second action identification (NeurIPS Datasets and Benchmarks 2022); overlap: Segments rehabilitation primitives (reach, transport, reposition) in stroke patients from video/IMU; directly competes with the phase-segmentation component and has real patient data, which the idea lacks.; verified=False
- On the analysis of movement smoothness (SPARC) (Journal of NeuroEngineering and Rehabilitation 2015); overlap: Defines one of the proposed metrics; not novelty-threatening but sets the standard that smoothness metrics are sensitive to noise, which single RGB-D depth noise will inflate.; verified=False
- The accuracy of several pose estimation methods for 3D joint centre localisation (Scientific Reports 2021); overlap: Shows keypoint joint centres are biased relative to anatomical centres; motivates the functional-calibration component but also means reviewers already know the bias exists.; verified=False

Could not be checked: the web search budget was exhausted.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: Make one paper with one claim: "Trustworthy single-RGB-D upper-limb kinematics: per-trial calibrated intervals and principled abstention for consensus stroke metrics." Use the SRRR-recommended drinking task or a reach-to-grasp task, not a custom cube transfer. The tagged object (a cup with an unobtrusive fiducial) is only an enabler for event timing. Validate against optical mocap in a partner gait or motion lab (rent time, even for 15-20 healthy adults), and add at least a small stroke cohort before submitting. Report ICC, SEM and MDC per metric, empirical coverage of 90 percent intervals, and coverage-versus-abstention curves. Show that abstention removes exactly the trials where markerless error exceeds the MDC. Compare with Azure Kinect body tracking, MediaPipe/RTMPose+depth and a multi-view markerless system. Spin the functional-calibration and digital-twin placement work out as separate, smaller papers (J Biomech technical note; EMBC or Sensors). Target JNER or TNSRE for the main paper, and npj Digital Medicine only after a multi-session patient cohort. For an ML venue (ML4H, CHIL), the novelty would have to be the conformal/abstention method for structured kinematic chains under missing measurements, evaluated on public mocap datasets as well.

## I17: Estimating trunk compensation during seated reaching when the desk hides the pelvis

Direction: Robust measurement of compensatory trunk motion, the most widely used compensation marker after stroke, with a single front-facing RGB-D camera at a table.

Research question: When a table occludes the hips, can anterior trunk displacement and trunk rotation during seated reaching be estimated from one RGB-D camera with errors below clinically relevant thresholds (a few cm and a few degrees), by replacing the hip-based torso frame with a chair-anchored, chest-surface-fitted torso model, and can the system flag the co-shift failures that fool landmark-only detectors?

Why novel (as proposed): Citations from memory, unverified; a 2024-2026 RGB-D trunk-compensation search (TNSRE, Sensors) is required. Trunk displacement is a core compensation metric in the Kwakkel et al. 2019 consensus; earlier work detected compensation with pressure mats (Cai et al., JNER 2019) or Kinect skeletons. Markerless pipelines build the torso from hip landmarks, which a table hides; the thesis showed the depth map then returns the desk and co-shifting hips fool every failure detector. Generic whole-body estimators (WHAM CVPR 2024, TRAM ECCV 2024) are not validated on this. Delta: a torso estimator not needing the pelvis, using a chair reference fitted once, a chest-surface fit from depth, shoulders and a sternum proxy, with an explicit observability label and a co-shift detector, validated with a controlled compensation-elicitation protocol.

Method (as proposed): Inputs: D435 depth and RGB, desk and gravity frames, a calibration frame of the empty chair. Representation: 6-DoF torso pose relative to a chair-anchored frame, observed by (a) the two shoulder landmarks back-projected with depth; (b) a chest-surface patch between the shoulders above the desk line, fitted as a plane or low-order surface by robust least squares (trunk normal and sternum depth); (c) an optional chair-back tag to detect chair movement. Estimator: the v3 tangent-space Kalman smoother extended with a pelvis-fixed-to-seat prior; pelvis translation bounded by the seat; trunk flexion as rotation about a hip axis estimated once in an upright calibration pose with hips visible. Honesty: measured when chest patch and both shoulders are valid, constrained when one shoulder is lost. Co-shift detector: shoulders moving while the chest plane does not flags a landmark error. Metrics: peak anterior trunk displacement (cm), trunk axial rotation (deg), trunk contribution to reach extent.

Evaluation (as proposed): Compensation elicitation: healthy subjects reach to targets at 80, 100 and 120 percent of arm length with and without an elbow brace restricting extension (graded ground truth). Reference: sternum and pelvis IMUs, a short borrowed mocap session if possible, tape-measured sternum-to-desk distance at static postures. About 20 healthy adults, then a stroke pilot. Baselines: thesis hip-based torso, MediaPipe world landmarks, RTMPose plus depth, WHAM or similar. Metrics: RMSE and Bland-Altman, detection AUC for compensated vs uncompensated reaches, false-compensation rate. Ablations: without chest-surface term, seat prior or co-shift detector; camera height and angle variations simulated in the Unity sensor-POV setup.

Target venues: IEEE Transactions on Neural Systems and Rehabilitation Engineering, Journal of Biomechanics, IEEE Journal of Biomedical and Health Informatics

Builds on the thesis: The thesis documented this failure (desk-defaulted hip depth, remembered-depth workaround, co-shifting hips); the metric world frame and gravity marker give trunk flexion relative to true vertical; the v3 quaternion core and Kalman smoother provide the estimator; Unity sensor-POV capture tests camera placements before data collection. Solving it early also protects the trunk metric in I16.

Feasibility (as proposed): Existing hardware plus an elbow brace and a few IMUs (under a few hundred dollars) or borrowed. Healthy-adult ethics is minimal risk. Timeline: months 0-2 estimator on existing and self-recordings; months 2-5 healthy study; months 5-7 paper; stroke pilot if a clinical partner is available.

Risks (as proposed): A healthy elbow-brace protocol may be judged an insufficient proxy for post-stroke compensation (which includes scapular elevation and lateral flexion a front camera sees poorly); chest-surface fit is sensitive to loose clothing and breast tissue (sex-dependent bias to analyse); may be seen as an engineering fix unless false-compensation-rate results are strong.

### novelty skeptic: score 3, refuted=True

Search could not be performed: the session's web-search budget was exhausted (200/200) before any query in this task ran, so no 2023-2026 paper could be verified. Four queries were attempted (RGB-D trunk compensation seated reaching stroke 2024; markerless trunk displacement Kinect Azure validation; compensation detection depth camera occlusion table torso; elbow-brace simulated compensation in healthy subjects) and all were refused. By the default rule (uncertain and no evidence either way -> refuted), and on substance from memory, the idea is weak for a top venue. (1) The application is crowded. Detecting trunk compensation during seated reaching from a front-facing depth camera has been studied since the Kinect v2 era. From memory, unverified: the Toronto Rehab Stroke Pose dataset (Dolatabadi et al., about 2017) has Kinect skeletons of stroke patients reaching at a table with frame-level compensation labels (trunk lean, trunk rotation, shoulder elevation), and several later papers (e.g. Zhi et al., IEEE JBHI about 2018-2020, real-time compensation detection during robotic therapy; Cai et al., JNER 2019 pressure mats; many 2021-2025 Sensors/TNSRE/JNER papers using OpenPose, MediaPipe or Azure Kinect) report detection accuracy or validity against mocap for trunk flexion and rotation. Most of these already use only shoulder and spine landmarks, or a seated-trunk model, rather than hip-based torso frames, because the table occludes the pelvis in every seated-table setup. The claimed gap ("markerless pipelines build the torso from hips") therefore holds for the thesis pipeline, not for the field. (2) The method delta is incremental engineering: a chair-anchored frame, a fixed-pelvis prior, a chest-plane fit from depth and a consistency check between shoulders and chest plane. Each is a standard component: seat-fixed pelvis assumptions are routine in seated biomechanics, and fitting chest or back planes to depth is common in posture monitoring. The co-shift detector is a cross-modal consistency check, which reviewers will not see as a contribution on its own. (3) The evaluation is a healthy-subject elbow-brace simulation with IMU reference. Simulated compensation in healthy adults with movement restriction is an established validation protocol and is known to be a weak proxy for post-stroke patterns (scapular elevation, lateral flexion), as the idea itself concedes. (4) The target venues (TNSRE, J Biomech, JBHI) are reasonable journals but not top CV/robotics venues. Even there, an n=20 healthy validation of one more depth-based trunk estimator would compete with existing Azure Kinect and markerless validity studies. Net: the novelty rests on a gap that is probably filled already, and none of it could be checked. Score 3/10.
