Suggested reframe: The idea will not survive as "a torso estimator that does not need the pelvis". Reposition the contribution as observability-aware trunk-compensation measurement with a calibrated false-compensation rate. Three concrete parts. (1) Benchmark existing depth and markerless trunk estimators (Azure Kinect body tracking, MediaPipe, RTMPose plus depth, WHAM) on public seated-reaching data such as the Toronto Rehab Stroke Pose set, plus a new synchronized RGB-D + IMU + mocap dataset with graded elicited compensation. Show quantitatively how often table occlusion and co-shifting landmarks produce false compensation labels. (2) Contribute a per-frame observability and uncertainty output, the measured / constrained / held flags carried over from the thesis, and show it lowers false-compensation alarms at a fixed sensitivity. The publishable claim is trustworthy clinical labels, not a lower RMSE. (3) Release the dataset and the evaluation protocol. Target JNER or TNSRE as a dataset-plus-validation paper. Before committing, run the literature check this session could not: 2023-2026 Azure Kinect/MediaPipe trunk-compensation validity studies in TNSRE, JNER, Sensors and JBHI. If any already reports table-occlusion-specific failure analysis, drop the idea.

### feasibility skeptic: score 5, refuted=False

Feasibility verdict: buildable with the stated resources and a 12 to 18 month timeline, but the evaluation as written cannot validate the headline metric. A narrower version with a cheap independent displacement reference is worth doing. Web search was unavailable in this run (session search budget exhausted), so no 2024-2026 RGB-D trunk-compensation paper was checked and the novelty claim stays unverified.

What is feasible. One D435, the desk and gravity markers, the v3 quaternion core and Kalman smoother, and a Unity sensor-POV setup are enough to build the estimator. The thesis confirms the failure being targeted: hip depth defaults to the desk or rail (thesis lines around 1776-1778, 3354-3355, depth 0.99 m sampled on the rail in front of the pelvis), and the torso is one rigid plate that cannot separate pelvis from upper trunk (line 1268). A healthy-adult elbow-brace study is minimal-risk and quick to approve. Twenty subjects in 3 months is realistic. Two sternum and pelvis IMUs plus a brace cost a few hundred dollars. RTMPose and MediaPipe baselines already run in v3.

Where the plan does not hold up:
(1) Ground truth for the main metric. Peak anterior trunk displacement is measured in cm. IMUs give orientation, not displacement. Double-integrated IMU displacement drifts by several cm within one reach, which is the same size as the error being measured. Indoor magnetometers also make axial-rotation references unreliable to a few degrees. Tape measurements cover static postures only. The mocap session is only "if possible", with no named source. As written, the dynamic displacement error has no reference. This is the kind of evaluation gap the lens refutes for, and it can be fixed cheaply (see reframe).
(2) A strong trivial baseline. The clinical metric is usually sternum or shoulder-midpoint displacement from the start position, not a pelvis-based quantity. Both shoulders and the upper chest stay above the desk and visible to a front camera. A plain mid-shoulder depth trajectory in the desk frame may already reach 1-2 cm and the same braced-vs-unbraced AUC. The pelvis is only needed to split trunk flexion from pelvis sliding or tilt. The proposed fix assumes that split away with a pelvis-fixed-to-seat prior and a hip axis calibrated once, and real compensation (anterior pelvic tilt, sliding forward on the seat) breaks exactly that assumption. The contribution could shrink to a small engineering refinement.
(3) Sensing physics. At about 1 m, D435 depth noise is around 1-2 cm per pixel. A plane fit over hundreds of chest pixels averages most of that out, but systematic bias from clothing, breast tissue and the reaching arm crossing the chest patch (contralateral and midline targets) does not average out. The proposal names this risk but includes no measurement of it.
(4) Clinical validity and venue. With no named clinical partner, the paper rests on the healthy brace proxy. TNSRE and JBHI accept healthy-subject validation, but reviewers will note that post-stroke compensation also involves scapular elevation and lateral flexion, which this design measures poorly. These are top rehabilitation-engineering venues, not the top CV or robotics venues the user is aiming for.
(5) Engineering risk is moderate, not dominant. The chair-anchored frame, chest fit and co-shift detector are each small. The risk is scientific (no measurable gain over the baseline), not whether the system can be built.

Minimum viable experiment (about 6-8 weeks):
- 5-8 healthy adults at the thesis desk; reach targets at 80, 100 and 120 percent of arm length, ipsilateral, midline and contralateral; with and without an elbow brace.
- Independent reference: a rigid sternum plate with an ArUco marker (or a small cube) seen by a second, separately calibrated camera from the side, registered to the same desk marker. This reuses the thesis marker branch (0.26 cm median on the rail test). Optionally add a cable-extension potentiometer from the chair back to the sternum for 1D displacement, plus a pelvis IMU to log pelvic tilt and catch failures of the seat prior.
- Compare: mid-shoulder depth baseline, thesis hip-based torso, MediaPipe world landmarks, RTMPose plus depth, and the proposed chest-surface plus seat-prior estimator.
- Report: RMSE and Bland-Altman for peak displacement and trunk flexion; AUC for braced vs unbraced; false-compensation rate on natural and injected co-shift failures; bias split by clothing and sex.
- Go/no-go: continue only if the proposed estimator beats the mid-shoulder baseline by a clinically meaningful margin (for example, more than 1 cm RMSE or a clearly lower false-compensation rate). Otherwise publish the validation and the failure taxonomy rather than the estimator.

Prior work found:
- Standardized measurement of quality of upper limb movement after stroke: consensus-based core recommendations from the Second Stroke Recovery and Rehabilitation Roundtable (Neurorehabilitation and Neural Repair 2019); overlap: Defines trunk displacement as a core kinematic compensation metric. This is where the target quantity comes from (sternum-based displacement, not pelvis-based), which weakens the need for a pelvis estimate.; verified=False
- Detecting compensatory movements of stroke survivors using pressure distribution data and machine learning algorithms (Journal of NeuroEngineering and Rehabilitation 2019); overlap: Detects trunk compensation without vision, using a seat pressure mat. It is a cheap alternative sensor and a likely reviewer baseline for the seat-anchored prior.; verified=False
- WHAM: Reconstructing World-grounded Humans with Accurate 3D Motion (CVPR 2024) https://github.com/yohanshin/WHAM; overlap: Generic world-grounded body estimator proposed as a baseline. It is not validated for seated, table-occluded trunk metrics. Not re-verified in this run because search was unavailable.; verified=False
- TRAM: Global Trajectory and Motion of 3D Humans from in-the-wild Videos (ECCV 2024); overlap: Another generic whole-body baseline named in the idea; same gap as WHAM.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: Reframe from a new pelvis-free torso estimator to a validation study of measuring trunk compensation with one RGB-D camera when a table hides the hips. The study benchmarks the plain mid-shoulder and sternum-proxy baseline, landmark-only methods (MediaPipe, RTMPose plus depth, WHAM-class) and a chest-surface plus seat-prior estimator against an independent side-camera sternum-marker reference (plus an optional cable potentiometer and a pelvis IMU). Healthy adults reach with and without an elbow brace. The headline result is the false-compensation rate caused by co-shift and desk-depth failures, and how an observability label and co-shift check reduce it, rather than raw RMSE. The pelvis model stays an ablation (does a seat prior help, and when does pelvic tilt break it). This needs about $300 of extra hardware (a second camera or webcam, a sternum plate, a brace, one IMU), keeps the ground truth independent of the camera under test, gives a publishable outcome even if the estimator does not beat the baseline, and targets TNSRE or JBHI. A stroke pilot is added only if a clinical partner is secured.

### venue skeptic: score 3, refuted=True

Area-chair view for TNSRE / J Biomech / JBHI. Note: the web search budget was used up in this session, so no 2024-2026 paper could be checked by search, and every citation below is verified=false.

(1) The problem is mostly caused by the thesis's own design, and that undercuts the headline. In clinical kinematics, anterior trunk displacement during seated reaching is the movement of a sternum (or shoulder-midpoint) marker relative to its starting position or the chair. This is how the Levin and Michaelsen lines of work measure it, and how the Kwakkel 2019 consensus defines the metric. It never needed a pelvis-based torso frame. Most Kinect and OpenPose compensation detectors already use shoulder or spine-shoulder displacement. A reviewer will say that hidden hips break the thesis's hip-anchored torso frame, but not the clinical metric. Once the hip-based baseline is removed, the remaining contribution is a chest-plane fit plus a seat prior, which is incremental engineering.

(2) Simpler fixes go unanswered. An elevated or oblique camera sees the pelvis. A pressure mat or a seat-back sensor measures trunk lean directly; Cai et al. 2019 used pressure distribution. One sternum IMU is cheap and accurate. The proposal treats a front camera at desk height as the only option without showing why that constraint holds clinically.

(3) Prior work is crowded and not mapped. There are Kinect-based compensation detection papers (for example Zhi et al., IEEE JTEHM 2018, from memory) and the Toronto Rehab Stroke Pose dataset (Dolatabadi et al. 2017). That dataset has Kinect recordings of stroke survivors with labelled trunk lean, trunk rotation and shoulder elevation during robotic reaching. There are also many 2D-pose compensation classifiers in Sensors and JNER, and Azure Kinect validity studies for trunk angles. The idea names none of these as baselines and compares only against the author's own hip-based frame plus generic estimators. WHAM and TRAM are the wrong comparators for a seated, partly occluded clinical reach.

(4) The evaluation is weak for TNSRE. The ground truth is healthy subjects in an elbow brace with IMUs, plus mocap only "if possible", plus tape measures. Reviewers routinely reject a brace proxy because post-stroke compensation includes scapular elevation, lateral flexion and abnormal synergies. The stroke pilot depends on a clinical partner who is not yet secured. Without an optical mocap reference, sub-cm and sub-degree accuracy claims will not be accepted. The chest-surface fit has a sex- and clothing-dependent bias that is acknowledged but not addressed.

(5) The co-shift detector and observability labels are the only distinctive part. They are a narrow add-on, not a headline. The idea has no single clear claim ("we reduce false-compensation rate by X under occlusion" is the closest), and nothing like it is shown yet.

Fit: TNSRE is the right journal, but this would read as a technical note there. As specified, it is more realistic for Sensors, JNER (only with a stroke cohort), or the IEEE EMBC / ICORR conferences. It is not a top-venue paper.

Prior work found:
- Standardized measurement of quality of upper limb movement after stroke: consensus-based core recommendations from the Second Stroke Recovery and Rehabilitation Roundtable (Kwakkel et al.) (Neurorehabilitation and Neural Repair 2019); overlap: Defines trunk displacement as a core kinematic compensation metric, measured from a sternum or trunk marker; no pelvis frame needed, so the occlusion premise is weakened; verified=False
- Detecting compensatory movements of stroke survivors using pressure distribution data and machine learning algorithms (Cai et al.) (Journal of NeuroEngineering and Rehabilitation 2019); overlap: Measures trunk compensation directly with a seat or back pressure sensor, a cheap alternative that is not affected by table occlusion; verified=False
- Automatic detection of compensation during robotic stroke rehabilitation therapy (Zhi et al.) (IEEE Journal of Translational Engineering in Health and Medicine 2018); overlap: Kinect-skeleton detection of trunk lean, trunk rotation and shoulder elevation in seated stroke reaching; a direct baseline the idea omits; verified=False
- The Toronto Rehab Stroke Pose Dataset to detect compensation during stroke rehabilitation therapy (Dolatabadi et al.) (PervasiveHealth 2017); overlap: Public Kinect dataset of stroke reaching with labelled trunk compensation; an obvious benchmark the proposal does not use; verified=False
- WHAM: Reconstructing World-grounded Humans with Accurate 3D Motion (CVPR 2024) https://github.com/yohanshin/WHAM; overlap: Generic whole-body estimator listed as a baseline; a weak comparator for a seated, occluded clinical reach; verified=False

Suggested reframe: Drop "estimating the torso without the pelvis" as the headline. Reframe the work as a validity and failure-mode study: "When do markerless RGB-D compensation detectors report false trunk compensation in seated table-top rehab, and can observability-aware gating remove it?"

Headline claim: off-the-shelf pipelines (MediaPipe, RTMPose plus depth, Azure Kinect Body Tracking) produce a measurable false-compensation rate when the desk occludes the body, the hands cross the chest, or the camera geometry changes. A sternum-and-chest-plane measure with co-shift and observability gating cuts that rate while keeping sensitivity.

Requirements for TNSRE or JNER:
- Optical mocap as the reference; borrow a lab session rather than relying on IMUs and tape.
- The clinically standard sternum-displacement metric as the primary outcome, not a pelvis-free 6-DoF torso.
- Every published Kinect and 2D-pose compensation baseline, plus evaluation on the Toronto Rehab Stroke Pose dataset.
- Bland-Altman against the minimal clinically important difference.
- At least a small stroke cohort, ideally 10 or more.

Without a stroke cohort and mocap, target Sensors or IEEE EMBC / ICORR instead. Alternatively, fold this into I16 as a robustness component rather than a standalone paper.

## I18: Object-mediated inter-limb coordination metrics for bimanual handovers in Parkinson disease and aging

Direction: Quantifying bimanual coordination deficits through object-mediated timing and spatial coupling, measured from a single RGB-D camera.

Research question: In a seated hand-to-hand object handover task, do object-mediated coordination metrics measured from one RGB-D camera (bimanual overlap time, giver-release to receiver-grasp lag, receiver anticipatory reach onset relative to giver transport peak, inter-wrist relative-phase variability) show good test-retest reliability, and do they separate people with Parkinson disease from age-matched controls better than unimanual speed metrics?

Why novel (as proposed): Citations from memory, unverified; check 2024-2026 TNSRE, JNER and Movement Disorders work. Video-based Parkinson assessment concentrates on unimanual MDS-UPDRS items and whole-body bradykinesia (npj Digital Medicine 2023; npj Parkinson's Disease 2023). Bimanual coordination deficits in PD are documented with motion capture or force sensors, but no known single-camera system measures the handover itself with object pose as the coupling variable. Krebs and Asfour (IROS 2024) formalize bimanual temporal and spatial constraints for robotics, not clinical measurement. The thesis measures object pose and both wrists in one metric frame during handovers, the precondition no hand-only markerless pipeline meets, since the hands occlude each other and the object is the only reliably observed coupling.

Method (as proposed): Task: cube starts on a left pad, picked by the left hand, handed to the right at midline, placed on a right pad, then reversed; 10 repetitions per direction at self-selected and fast speed. Inputs: the two-branch pipeline giving both wrists, both elbows and object 6-DoF in the desk frame with honesty labels. Events with the object as reference: per-hand grip state by hysteresis on wrist-to-object distance in the object frame using the per-hand grip offset; bimanual-contact interval when both are true; giver release when the giver's grip offset becomes non-rigid (variance rises); the I01 mode inference can be reused. Metrics: overlap duration, release lag, receiver reach onset vs giver transport velocity peak, continuous relative phase of the wrist speed profiles, object orientation change during the handover (fumble indicator). When a wrist is occluded at the handover, object-conditioned recovery places it; metrics relying on recovered wrists are flagged and reported with and without those frames. Statistics: linear mixed models with group and speed effects; ICC for test-retest.

Evaluation (as proposed): Cohorts: 15-20 PD (ON medication), 15-20 age-matched controls, 15-20 young adults, with a second session within a week. Reference: borrowed forearm IMUs for timing plus force-sensitive resistor or capacitive strips on the cube faces for grasp/release truth. No public PD handover dataset is known; OakInk2 and ARCTIC only sanity-check the event detector on healthy data. Metrics: event timing error vs touch sensors, ICC(2,1), effect sizes and AUC for PD vs control, correlation with MDS-UPDRS Part III upper-limb items rated by a clinician. Baselines: unimanual movement time and peak speed; hand-only event detection without object pose; RTMPose 2D-only metrics. Ablations: with/without object-conditioned recovery; causal (v2) vs offline (v1), given the thesis finding that the two can diverge after a twist freeze.

Target venues: IEEE Transactions on Neural Systems and Rehabilitation Engineering, Journal of NeuroEngineering and Rehabilitation, npj Parkinson's Disease

Builds on the thesis: The handover recordings and per-hand grip-offset estimate are the core of the event detector; object-conditioned recovery handles mutual hand occlusion; both arms and the object are in one metric world frame; honesty labels show which coordination metrics depend on inferred frames.

Feasibility (as proposed): About 70 percent of the software exists; new code is the event detector, metrics and statistics. Needed: ethics application and a PD clinical partner (main bottleneck), touch-sensor instrumentation of the cube (one week of electronics), borrowed IMUs, about 60 sessions. Timeline: months 0-2 protocol, ethics and pilot on existing recordings plus 5 healthy volunteers; months 2-8 recruitment; months 8-12 analysis and paper. A healthy young vs older adults paper is possible earlier.

Risks (as proposed): Small single-site cohort with no prior evidence handover metrics are clinically meaningful in PD; reviewers will ask for correlation with clinical scales and medication-state effects; a tagged cube and fixed camera look like a lab rig; PD tremor and motion blur degrade ArUco detection exactly where the object branch is most needed.

### novelty skeptic: score 4, refuted=True

Evidence status: the session web-search budget was already exhausted (every WebSearch call returned "Web search was not performed: 200 of 200 calls used"), so no 2023-2026 paper could be checked in this pass. All prior work below comes from memory and is marked verified=false. Under the task rule (refuted=true when uncertain and no evidence either way), the verdict is refuted=true. The substantive reasons below would hold even with full search.
(1) The clinical construct is not new. Bimanual coordination deficits in PD are well documented in motor-control work: Swinnen, Serrien and colleagues on bimanual phase coupling, relative-phase variability and anti-phase instability in PD, and grip-force coordination studies of bimanual and hand-to-hand object manipulation. Continuous relative phase, inter-limb lag and release/grasp timing are standard metrics there. What remains is measuring a known construct with a new sensor, which is incremental for TNSRE/JNER and not enough for npj Parkinson's Disease without clinical validity at scale.
(2) The sensing is close to existing work. Single depth-camera (Kinect / Azure Kinect) and video pipelines for PD upper-limb MDS-UPDRS items, with ICC test-retest studies, are a crowded TNSRE/JNER/Sensors topic. Islam et al. (npj Digit Med 2023) and Morinan et al. (npj PD 2023) are the cited exemplars. Automated Box-and-Block and ARAT scoring with Kinect or video plus hand-object interaction (stroke, 2013-2024) already uses object-mediated events (grasp, transport, release) from one camera. Moving that to a two-hand handover is a narrow step.
(3) The claimed differentiator is weak for clinical reviewers. That differentiator is object pose as the coupling variable under mutual hand occlusion. It depends on an ArUco-tagged cube, and the risk list itself says PD tremor and blur degrade ArUco detection, which undercuts the point. Reviewers will ask why an instrumented object (IMU plus FSR inside the cube) is not used directly. That object is already the proposed reference, so the camera may be redundant for the clinical question.
(4) The headline hypothesis is unsupported. "Bimanual handover metrics separate PD from controls better than unimanual speed" has no pilot evidence. With 15-20 per group, single site, ON medication only, a null or modest AUC is likely, and that would not reach a top venue.
(5) Krebs and Asfour (IROS 2024) is robotics taxonomy and not a real competitor. Citing it does not establish clinical novelty.
Net: the exact combination (single RGB-D, handover, object-pose-anchored events, honesty-flagged recovered wrists) is plausibly unpublished. That novelty is a narrow combination, though, and could not be verified. For the stated venues the delta rests on an unproven clinical effect rather than a methodological contribution.

Prior work found:
- Using AI to measure Parkinson's disease severity at home (finger-tapping video analysis) (npj Digital Medicine 2023); overlap: Single-camera video quantification of PD motor signs with clinical-score correlation; unimanual, no object, but sets the baseline that video PD assessment is established.; verified=False
- Computer vision quantification of whole-body Parkinsonian bradykinesia using a large multi-site population (npj Parkinson's Disease 2023); overlap: Markerless pose-based PD severity with multi-site cohort; shows the scale (hundreds of patients) that top clinical venues now expect, against the proposed 15-20.; verified=False
- Formalization of temporal and spatial constraints of bimanual manipulation categories (Krebs, Asfour) (IROS 2024); overlap: Bimanual temporal and spatial coupling taxonomy for robotics; supplies coordination categories but no clinical measurement. Not a competitor.; verified=False
- Swinnen, Serrien et al., bimanual coordination and phase-coupling deficits in Parkinson's disease (series of motor-control studies) (Neuropsychologia / Exp Brain Res / Brain, 2000s-2010s); overlap: Already establishes relative-phase variability and inter-limb coupling deficits in PD, which are the core proposed metrics, measured with motion capture or manipulanda.; verified=False
- Kinect / Azure Kinect based automated Box and Block Test and upper-limb MDS-UPDRS item scoring, with ICC reliability (TNSRE / JNER / Sensors, 2013-2024 (several papers)); overlap: Single depth camera, object-mediated grasp-transport-release event detection, test-retest ICC and clinical validity; the handover is a two-hand variant of this design.; verified=False
- Grip-force coordination during bimanual object manipulation and hand-to-hand transfer in Parkinson's disease (instrumented-object studies) (Exp Brain Res / Mov Disord era studies, pre-2023); overlap: Instrumented objects already measure giver-release and receiver-grasp timing in PD directly; this is the reference the proposal itself plans to use, which raises the question of why the camera is needed.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: Recast this as a measurement-methods paper (TNSRE/JNER) and drop the clinical-discrimination claim as the headline. Proposed claim: object-anchored event detection from a single RGB-D camera recovers bimanual handover timing (release lag, overlap, receiver reach onset) to within X ms of an instrumented-object reference under mutual hand occlusion. Hand-only markerless pipelines fail on the same frames. Honesty-flagged recovered wrists quantify which metrics remain valid.
Evaluation plan:
- Validate first on healthy young and older adults (no clinical partner needed, about 30-40 people).
- Include an untagged-object variant using a model-based 6-DoF tracker such as FoundationPose, so reviewers do not dismiss the method as an ArUco lab rig.
- Report PD only as a small pilot feasibility cohort with effect sizes, not AUC claims.
Before investing, rerun the novelty search the budget blocked, using these terms: "bimanual handover Parkinson kinematics", "hand-to-hand transfer older adults inter-limb", "Azure Kinect Box and Block automated", "markerless bimanual coordination PD 2024 2025".

### feasibility skeptic: score 3, refuted=True

Feasibility verdict: refuted as written. The software and recording assets are real, but the clinical study rests on a resource the idea does not say how to get, and the core measurement is circular exactly where the metrics need to be measured. (1) Missing resource. A PD clinical partner, an ethics approval and a clinician to rate MDS-UPDRS are called "the main bottleneck", but nothing says how to obtain them. The dossier lists one camera, one subject and no clinical affiliation. Recruiting 15-20 ON-medication PD participants plus two age-matched groups for about 60 two-session visits in months 2-8 is optimistic even with a partner. Without one, the headline result (PD vs control AUC, correlation with UPDRS) cannot be produced inside a 12-18 month student project. (2) The event detector cannot resolve the events the metrics name. The thesis grip state (Section 5, holding-state rules) uses a 25 cm hold radius, a 35 cm release radius and five-frame confirmation runs, which is about 167 ms at 30 fps. In a midline handover both wrists sit inside 25 cm of the cube centre for the whole transfer, so "bimanual overlap time" would mostly measure the hysteresis, not the grasp. Release lag in healthy handovers is on the order of a few hundred milliseconds, and group differences would be tens of milliseconds. Detecting them needs finger-level contact, but the pipeline tracks the wrist as a point with no fingers or orientation (thesis limitations). (3) Circularity at the handover. Mutual hand occlusion at the midline is exactly when the wrists fail. Object-conditioned recovery then places the wrist from the object pose plus a grip offset assumed rigid. The proposed release detector ("giver release when the grip offset becomes non-rigid") therefore relies on wrist positions that, in occluded frames, were built by assuming the offset is rigid. The idea's plan to report metrics "with and without recovered frames" may leave few or no measured frames in the handover window. The thesis itself reports a stale grip offset defeating recovery on one natural window, and causal and offline passes diverging by 12 cm after a twist freeze. (4) Object branch fragility. With hands wrapped around the cube at the handover, at fast speed, and with PD tremor, ArUco corners are occluded or blurred just when the object is the only coupling variable. The thesis measured no orientation reference, so the "fumble indicator" (orientation change) has no validated accuracy. (5) Ground truth is plausible but undercuts the contribution. FSR or capacitive strips on the cube faces compete for the surfaces that carry the markers. If they are added, the instrumented cube alone gives better grasp and release timing than the camera, so a reviewer will ask what the camera adds beyond reach-onset and inter-wrist phase. (6) Venue fit. TNSRE and JNER accept small-cohort pilot reliability studies, but with n=15-20 per group, a single site and no medication-state contrast, the PD discrimination claim is weak. These are also not the CV or robotics top venues the user is aiming for. What is feasible: healthy young vs older adults, which needs no clinical partner, and the reuse of about 70 percent of the code is real. Literature: the web search budget for this session was used up before any query ran, so none of the cited prior works could be checked. All are marked unverified, and novelty against 2024-2026 TNSRE, JNER and Movement Disorders work is unknown. Minimum viable experiment (about 3-4 months, no clinical partner): 10-12 healthy young adults and, if possible, 8-10 older adults. Use a cube with the existing ArUco faces plus capacitive touch pads on two opposite faces, logged by a microcontroller synchronized to the RealSense via an LED flash visible in RGB. Each person does 20 handovers per direction at two speeds over two sessions. Primary outcomes: (a) camera-derived giver-release and receiver-grasp times vs touch truth, reported as median and p95 timing error and the fraction of handovers in which the wrist is measured rather than recovered inside a ±200 ms window around the event; (b) ICC(2,1) between sessions for overlap duration, release lag and reach onset; (c) the same metrics from an event detector redesigned around object kinematics (onset of object velocity and acceleration changes, i.e. load transfer seen as an object motion transient) rather than wrist radius hysteresis. Pass criteria: timing error under about 50 ms for the object-kinematics events and ICC above 0.75. If these fail, the clinical study is not worth starting.

Prior work found:
- Using AI to measure Parkinson's disease severity at home (npj Digital Medicine 2023); overlap: Video-based PD severity scoring at home from unimanual MDS-UPDRS-style tasks (finger tapping); establishes the camera-based PD assessment baseline the idea positions against; no bimanual or object-mediated metrics as far as recalled.; verified=False
- Computer vision quantification of whole-body Parkinsonian bradykinesia using a large multi-site population (npj Parkinson's Disease 2023); overlap: Markerless whole-body bradykinesia quantification across sites; competes on the claim that single-camera PD metrics are clinically useful, and sets a large-cohort bar a 15-20 person single-site study cannot match.; verified=False
- Formalization of temporal and spatial constraints of bimanual manipulation categories (IROS 2024); overlap: Formal taxonomy of bimanual temporal and spatial coupling for robotics; supplies the vocabulary (coordination categories, timing constraints) but no clinical measurement.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: Step 1 (feasible now): "Measuring handover timing from a single RGB-D camera, using object motion as the coupling signal: validation against an instrumented object in healthy young and older adults." The contribution is a measurement-method paper. It shows that events detected from object kinematics (load-transfer transients in the 6-DoF object track) plus measured, not recovered, wrist reach onset match capacitive touch truth within a stated timing error and are reliable across sessions. It reports honestly what fraction of handover windows depend on recovered wrists. It drops the 25/35 cm radius hysteresis as the event source and adds a better hand model (RTMPose or MediaPipe Hands) as an ablation. Target: TNSRE or JNER methods, or an IEEE EMBC or BSN short paper first. Step 2, only after a clinical partner and ethics approval are in hand: a PD pilot as a separate follow-up. Do not start the clinical study until the step-1 pass criteria (event timing error under about 50 ms, ICC above 0.75) are met.

### venue skeptic: score 3, refuted=True

Web search was unavailable: the session search budget was used up, so no citation was verified and the venue check rests on prior knowledge. Area-chair view for TNSRE, JNER and npj Parkinson's Disease follows.

(1) The headline claim is weak. That bimanual coordination and relative-phase stability are impaired in PD is long established with motion capture and manipulanda. The new element is only the sensor, one RGB-D camera plus a tagged cube, so the paper reads as a measurement-validation study, not a clinical finding.

(2) The evaluation undercuts its own premise. Ground truth comes from an instrumented cube with FSR or capacitive strips plus forearm IMUs. Those sensors measure grasp, release, overlap and lag directly and more accurately than the camera, and they are cheaper and easier to deploy clinically than a calibrated D435 with three ArUco markers. Any reviewer will ask why the camera is needed when the reference instrument is already the better clinical tool.

(3) The camera's measurement validity is unproven. The thesis has one subject, cm-level wrist error and a recovery layer that failed on one of two natural-occlusion windows. Causal and offline runs diverged by 12 cm after a twist freeze. Timing metrics depend on a grip offset that the thesis itself reports going stale. In PD, tremor and motion blur degrade ArUco detection exactly at the handover.

(4) The cohort is small (15-20 per group, single site, ON medication only), with no OFF state and no longitudinal arm. AUC from about 35 people with many candidate metrics invites overfitting criticism. The ICC and UPDRS correlations would be underpowered.

(5) The baseline story is unbalanced. Unimanual movement time is a weak strawman. The fair baselines are the instrumented cube alone, wrist IMUs alone and existing video UPDRS scorers such as finger tapping and hand movements, and the camera would likely lose to the first two.

(6) It does not fit the user's stated goal of top conferences such as CVPR, CoRL or SIGGRAPH; it is a clinical-engineering journal paper at best. Without a clinical partner, ethics approval and recruitment, it is not feasible for an undergraduate-to-graduate transition on the stated timeline.

Expected outcome at TNSRE or npj Parkinson's Disease: reject or major revision. At JNER: possible with a stronger cohort. More realistic first venues are IEEE EMBC or IEEE JBHI or Sensors, as a healthy young-vs-older pilot.

Prior work found:
- Using AI to measure Parkinson's disease severity at home (npj Digital Medicine 2023); overlap: Video-based PD severity scoring from unimanual MDS-UPDRS tasks; this is the standard video baseline that reviewers will expect the handover metrics to beat on PD-vs-control separation and UPDRS correlation.; verified=False
- Computer vision quantification of whole-body Parkinsonian bradykinesia using a large multi-site population (npj Parkinson's Disease 2023); overlap: Markerless video quantification of PD motor signs with a multi-site cohort; sets the cohort-size and multi-site bar that a 15-20 per group single-site study will be compared against.; verified=False
- Formalization of temporal and spatial constraints of bimanual manipulation categories (IROS 2024); overlap: Formalizes bimanual temporal and spatial coupling categories for robotics; it offers a taxonomy, not clinical measurement, so it gives little support to the clinical novelty claim.; verified=False
- Classical motor-control literature on bimanual coordination deficits in PD (relative phase and anti-phase instability, e.g. Swinnen and colleagues), measured with motion capture or manipulanda (various, 2000s-2020s); overlap: Already establishes that relative-phase variability and inter-limb coupling are impaired in PD. That makes the clinical hypothesis largely expected; only the sensing modality is new.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: If kept at all, invert the framing. Make the instrumented cube plus a markerless camera (no ArUco tag) the system. Make the claim methodological: object-mediated event detection gives handover timing metrics that agree with contact sensors (ICC, Bland-Altman) even under mutual hand occlusion, where hand-only markerless pipelines fail. Validate first on healthy young vs older adults, plus OakInk2 and ARCTIC for the event detector. Target IEEE JBHI or EMBC, then JNER. Move the PD cohort to a follow-up study run with a clinical partner, with ON/OFF medication states and a pre-registered small metric set compared against wrist IMUs and existing video MDS-UPDRS hand-item scorers. For the top-conference goal, this direction should be dropped in favour of a vision or robotics contribution.

## I19: Only motion leaves the device: utility-preserving de-identification of third-person RGB-D kinematic streams for remote assessment

Direction: Privacy-preserving capture for telepresence and remote assessment: edge inference transmitting kinematics instead of video, and quantifying what the kinematic stream still reveals.

Research question: If an edge device transmits only a low-dimensional kinematic stream (torso pose, swing-twist angles, object pose, honesty flags), how identifiable is the person across sessions, which components carry identity (segment lengths, static posture, dynamics), and can a transformation remove re-identification risk while keeping assessment metrics (reach, range of motion, bimanual handover timing) within a stated tolerance?

Why novel (as proposed): Citations from memory, unverified. Nair et al. (USENIX Security 2023) showed VR head and hand telemetry identifies 50,000+ users; follow-up anonymization (Deep Motion Masking, venue uncertain) targets first-person VR trackers; privacy-preserving action recognition (Wu et al., TPAMI 2020) removes identity from video, not kinematic streams. Delta: third-person RGB-D upper-body plus object streams, utility defined by assessment metrics, and an explicit representation (effective segment lengths plus swing-twist angles) that separates anthropometry from motion. Body-motion anonymization may already be covered broadly; the third-person, object-coupled, assessment-utility framing must be checked against 2025-2026 PETS / USENIX / IEEE VR papers.

Method (as proposed): Pipeline: the causal chain on an edge device (Jetson Orin Nano/NX; RTMPose-m in TensorRT, ArUco in OpenCV); only the packet leaves the device. Attack: re-ID classifiers (temporal CNN or transformer, plus GBDT on handcrafted features) on kinematic packets; train/test on public multi-subject motion (AMASS or Human3.6M mapped to torso pose + 4 swing-twist angles per arm + segment lengths), lab recordings as real-sensor check. Defences: (1) anthropometry canonicalization (population-mean segment lengths, retarget through the explicit chain, closed form); (2) remove static posture offsets; (3) learned perturbation in swing-twist tangent space with a utility loss bounding assessment metrics and an adversarial privacy loss; (4) optional local differential privacy on summary statistics. Honesty flags still mark measured joints so the transform never makes held values look measured.

Evaluation (as proposed): Re-ID top-1 and EER across sessions before/after each defence; utility as error in assessment metrics vs the unmodified stream and vs tape/rail references; realism via a short perceptual study or motion-realism discriminator; edge fps and power. Baselines: raw 3D keypoints, kinematic stream without canonicalization, matched-utility Gaussian noise, a VR-telemetry anonymizer re-implementation if code exists. Ablations: identity carried by segment lengths only, static posture only, dynamics only.

Target venues: IEEE VR (TVCG track), IEEE ISMAR, PETS, IEEE TVCG

Builds on the thesis: The explicit swing-twist representation with effective segment lengths makes anthropometry a separable parameter; the causal chain proves the stream can be produced live; honesty flags make the transform auditable; the Unity twin reconstructs from the packet alone.

Feasibility (as proposed): Buy a Jetson Orin Nano kit (about USD 250-500). Main identification results from public multi-subject data; a consented 10-15-person recording (ethics approval) strengthens the real-sensor claim. Timeline: month 1 edge port; months 2-3 data conversion and attacks; months 4-5 defences and utility; month 6 writing.

Risks (as proposed): Privacy reviewers may see motion identifiability as established and the defence as a known pattern; XR reviewers may find utility metrics under-validated clinically; public mocap converted to the thesis representation lacks real RGB-D noise; single-subject lab data is thin; weakest link to the thesis's core contributions.

### novelty skeptic: score 3, refuted=True

Evidence limits first: no search returned any result. WebSearch had run out of budget (200 of 200 used before this agent started), and arxiv.org, export.arxiv.org and api.semanticscholar.org are all blocked by the egress proxy. Every prior work listed below therefore comes from memory and is marked verified=false. The brief says to default to refuted when the evidence is uncertain, and in any case the remembered prior art covers each part of the idea.

(1) Attack side. The research question asks how identifiable a person is from a low-dimensional kinematic stream and which parts carry identity. This is the main result of Nair et al. (USENIX Security 2023), which identified over 50,000 users from head and hand motion and found that anthropometric features such as height and wingspan carry much of the identity. Gait and skeleton identification work, for example Hanisch et al. on gait identification (PETS 2023), and the long line of skeleton-based person re-identification on Kinect or NTU skeletons, already covers third-person depth-sensor skeletons. Moving from first-person VR trackers to a third-person RGB-D skeleton is a change of sensor, not a new finding.

(2) Defence (1), canonicalising segment lengths, and defence (4), local differential privacy. MetaGuard (Nair et al., PETS 2023) already applies local differential privacy to VR telemetry, explicitly including anthropometric attributes. Normalising or retargeting bone lengths to a template skeleton is a standard step in skeleton anonymization and retargeting work. Doing it in closed form through the explicit chain is an implementation detail, not a contribution.

(3) Defence (3), a learned perturbation with a utility loss and an adversarial privacy loss. This is the setup of Moon et al., 'Anonymization for Skeleton Action Recognition' (AAAI 2023): an adversarially trained skeleton anonymizer that blocks re-identification while preserving action-recognition utility. Deep Motion Masking (Nair et al., about 2023, venue unconfirmed) does real-time learned anonymization of motion with usability checks. Wu et al. (TPAMI 2020) supplies the general privacy-versus-utility adversarial framework. Replacing the action-recognition loss with a loss on assessment metrics (reach, range of motion, handover timing) is a small change of task.

(4) The remaining differences are object pose in the stream, honesty flags, edge deployment on a Jetson, and clinical assessment metrics as the utility. These are systems or application details. They will not convince a PETS or USENIX reviewer, who will see motion identifiability as established. They will not convince an IEEE VR or TVCG reviewer either, who will see the clinical utility as unvalidated, with a single subject and public mocap converted to the thesis representation without real RGB-D noise.

(5) The idea is only weakly tied to the thesis's validated contributions. The thesis supplies a representation, not evidence on privacy.

Overall the novelty delta is too small for a top venue, so refuted=true and novelty is scored 3/10.

Prior work found:
- Unique Identification of 50,000+ Virtual Reality Users from Head and Hand Motion Data (USENIX Security 2023); overlap: Re-identification from low-dimensional motion telemetry at scale; feature analysis shows anthropometry (height, arm span) plus dynamics carry identity. Covers the attack half of the research question.; verified=False
- MetaGuard: Going Incognito in the Metaverse (PETS 2023); overlap: Local differential privacy on XR telemetry, including anthropometric attributes. Covers defence (4) and part of defence (1).; verified=False
- Deep Motion Masking for Secure, Usable, and Scalable Real-Time Anonymization of Virtual Reality Motion Data (arXiv about 2023 (venue unconfirmed)); overlap: Learned real-time motion anonymization that keeps motion usable, evaluated against re-ID attacks. Covers defence (3) for VR trackers.; verified=False
- Anonymization for Skeleton Action Recognition (AAAI 2023); overlap: Adversarially trained skeleton anonymizer (privacy loss against a re-ID model plus a utility loss for action recognition) on third-person depth skeletons. Matches defence (3) and the third-person skeleton setting almost exactly.; verified=False
- Understanding Person Identification Through Gait (PETS 2023); overlap: Identifiability of third-person skeleton and pose sequences and which components carry identity. Overlaps the ablation on segment lengths, static posture and dynamics.; verified=False
- Privacy-Preserving Deep Action Recognition: An Adversarial Learning Framework and A New Dataset (IEEE TPAMI 2020); overlap: General adversarial privacy-versus-utility framework that the learned defence would reuse.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: Only a narrower framing is likely to survive. Option A: present it as a measurement and leakage study of object-coupled bimanual streams. Ask whether identity and anthropometry leak back through the object-pose and handover channel after the body skeleton is canonicalised. Example: can segment lengths be recovered from object trajectories and reach envelopes? Then give a closed-form canonicalisation through the explicit swing-twist chain that keeps named clinical metrics provably unchanged, or bounded, rather than learning a perturbation. Test it against a real multi-subject RGB-D cohort, not converted mocap. Option B: fold privacy into a remote-assessment systems paper, for example for IEEE JBHI or a CHI health track. There the main contribution is validated assessment from kinematics-only transmission, and privacy is one evaluated property. Either way, first check Moon et al. (AAAI 2023), MetaGuard (PETS 2023) and any 2024-2026 follow-ups on skeleton anonymization; this agent could not run those searches.

### feasibility skeptic: score 5.5, refuted=False

Note: the web search budget for this session ran out before any search returned, so no prior work below is verified. Every citation comes from memory and is marked verified=false.

Feasibility verdict: the core computation is feasible for a small lab. The idea as written is not, because it chooses the wrong training data and rests its distinctive claims (object coupling, third-person RGB-D noise, bimanual handover utility, edge deployment) on resources it does not have.

What works. (1) Ground truth for privacy is free and exact: the dataset gives subject labels, so re-ID top-1 and EER need no annotation. (2) Utility ground truth is relative. Assessment metrics computed on the unmodified stream are the reference, so error after the defence needs no motion capture. (3) The thesis representation (torso frame, shoulder swing x2, twist, elbow flexion, effective segment lengths) can be computed in closed form from any 3D upper-body skeleton. The v3 unit-quaternion core already does this, so a conversion takes days, not months. (4) The attack models (temporal CNN or transformer, GBDT on handcrafted features) and the defences (bone-length canonicalization, posture-offset removal, a tangent-space perturbation trained with an adversary) train in hours on one RTX 3080. (5) None of this needs the Jetson, ethics approval or new recordings.

What breaks.
(a) Wrong data. AMASS is mocap with sparse repeated sessions per subject and almost no seated desk manipulation. Human3.6M has only 7 public subjects (S9 and S11 test labels are effectively held back), too few for a re-ID claim. Cross-session re-identification needs many subjects, each recorded more than once in different setups.
(b) The object-coupled and bimanual-handover utility has no data source. Only the author's own single-subject ArUco recordings contain object pose and handovers. Re-ID needs at least 2 people by definition, and a credible study needs dozens. The 10-15 person consented study needs ethics approval, a recording protocol and about 2-4 months, and it still gives too few identities for a strong EER claim.
(c) "Third-person RGB-D noise" is claimed, but converted mocap does not have it.
(d) The edge port (RTMPose in TensorRT on an Orin Nano, ArUco, live packetization) is engineering with no research payoff. The thesis never validated a live-camera session, so this is non-trivial risk on a path reviewers will not reward.
(e) Clinical assessment validity (reach, ROM, handover timing) cannot be shown with one subject and tape or rail references. XR and TNSRE reviewers will ask for it.

Fix for (a) to (c), from memory and unverified: NTU RGB+D 120 has 106 subjects, Kinect v2 third-person RGB-D skeletons, 32 setups and multiple camera views, so it has real depth-sensor noise and natural cross-setup splits. Its upper-body joints map directly to the thesis representation. Rehabilitation Kinect datasets such as KIMORE and UI-PRMD give assessment-style utility (exercise ROM, quality scores). With these the study is feasible in about 4-6 months without new recordings.

Novelty risk that affects feasibility. Skeleton anonymization for action recognition on NTU already exists (Moon et al., AAAI 2023, from memory). Motion-based identification and anonymization of VR telemetry exists (Nair et al. 2023; Deep Motion Masking). Bone-length normalization is a standard step. The feasible version is therefore incremental unless the decomposition result is sharp. That result would be the quantified identity share carried by anthropometry vs static posture vs dynamics, on a sensor-noisy third-person stream, under an assessment-utility constraint. This is a venue risk, not a resource risk. It caps the realistic target at IEEE VR / ISMAR / PETS short or workshop tier unless the decomposition finding is surprising.

Minimum viable experiment (about 8-10 weeks, no new hardware):
1. Convert NTU RGB+D 120 skeletons, upper body only, to the thesis packet: torso pose plus 4 angles per arm, plus segment lengths. Use the v3 math core.
2. Train re-ID attackers on cross-setup splits, with three input variants: raw 3D keypoints; the packet; the packet with segment lengths replaced by population means and static posture offsets removed.
3. Report top-1 and EER for each variant, plus an ablation that keeps only segment lengths, only static posture, or only dynamics.
4. Compute utility metrics on the original and transformed streams: per-joint ROM, reach envelope, and action-recognition accuracy as a proxy. Optionally use KIMORE exercise scores.
5. Compare against Gaussian noise at matched utility.

Success criterion: canonicalization plus offset removal gives a large EER increase (for example re-ID top-1 drops by more than half) with ROM error under a stated tolerance (for example under 3 deg), and dynamics-only identity is quantified. If dynamics alone still identifies people strongly, add the adversarial tangent-space perturbation as step 6. Use the author's own recordings only as a qualitative demo that the transformed packet still drives the Unity twin with honesty flags intact.

Overall: feasible if reframed around public third-person skeleton datasets. As written (AMASS/H36M, object-coupled bimanual utility, edge deployment, a new human-subject study) the plan underestimates data and ethics needs and spends effort on engineering that does not support the research claim.

Prior work found:
- Unique Identification of 50,000+ Virtual Reality Users from Head and Hand Motion Data (USENIX Security 2023); overlap: Shows motion telemetry identifies users at scale; establishes that kinematic streams are identifying, so the attack half of the idea is not novel.; verified=False
- Deep Motion Masking for Secure, Usable, and Scalable Real-Time Anonymization of Virtual Reality Motion Data (arXiv 2023 (Nair et al.; peer-reviewed venue unconfirmed)); overlap: Learned real-time anonymization of motion telemetry with a usability constraint; same defence pattern, applied to first-person VR trackers rather than third-person RGB-D skeletons.; verified=False
- Anonymization for Skeleton Action Recognition (AAAI 2023 (Moon et al.)); overlap: Adversarial anonymization of 3D skeletons (NTU RGB+D) trading re-ID against action-recognition utility; the closest third-person skeleton precedent, and it already uses Kinect RGB-D skeletons.; verified=False
- Privacy-Preserving Deep Action Recognition: An Adversarial Learning Framework and A New Dataset (IEEE TPAMI 2020 (Wu et al.)); overlap: Adversarial privacy/utility trade-off for action recognition, on video rather than kinematics.; verified=False
- NTU RGB+D 120: A Large-Scale Benchmark for 3D Human Activity Understanding (IEEE TPAMI 2020 (Liu et al.)); overlap: Not competing work: the 106-subject, multi-setup Kinect skeleton dataset that makes the feasible version possible without new recordings.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: "What identifies a person in a third-person upper-body kinematic stream, and how much can be removed at a fixed assessment utility?" This is a measurement-and-defence study on public Kinect skeleton data (NTU RGB+D 120 for cross-setup re-ID, KIMORE or UI-PRMD for rehabilitation-assessment utility), converted to the thesis representation: torso pose, swing-twist angles and effective segment lengths. Contributions: (1) an identity decomposition into anthropometry, static posture and dynamics; (2) a closed-form canonicalization through the explicit chain as the first defence; (3) an adversarially trained tangent-space perturbation only if dynamics still identify. Utility is ROM and reach error relative to the unmodified stream, with exercise-score agreement on the rehabilitation data. Drop the Jetson port, the object-coupled bimanual handover utility and the new consented study from the core claim. Keep the author's own recordings and the Unity twin as a demonstration that the transformed packet still drives reconstruction with honesty flags intact. Target IEEE VR/ISMAR poster or full paper, or a PETS short paper. A 10-15 person ethics-approved recording with object handling is an optional second phase if the first result is strong.

### venue skeptic: score 3, refuted=True

Area-chair view. Web search was unavailable in this run (the session's search budget was used up), so every citation below comes from memory and is marked unverified.

1. The headline claim is already known. Both privacy and XR reviewers accept that body-motion streams identify people: Nair et al. (USENIX Security 2023) identified 50k+ VR users from motion; the BOXRR-23 dataset and later work extend this; Rack et al. compared motion encodings for user identification; and skeleton-based gait and person re-identification is a mature CV topic. Showing that a third-person upper-body stream is also identifiable, and that segment lengths carry much of the identity, is expected, not surprising. "Segment lengths identify people" is close to a textbook point in anthropometric biometrics.

2. The defences are standard patterns:
- Canonicalising anthropometry to population-mean bone lengths is ordinary motion retargeting.
- Removing the static posture offset is mean subtraction.
- A learned perturbation with an adversarial privacy loss plus a utility loss copies the adversarial anonymisation template (Wu et al. TPAMI 2020 for video; Moon et al. AAAI 2023 for skeleton action recognition; Deep Motion Masking for VR telemetry).
- Local differential privacy on summary statistics is standard.
The only new element is defining utility through assessment metrics, and the idea does not establish those metrics as clinically meaningful.

3. The evaluation would not hold up:
- The re-identification results would come from AMASS or Human3.6M converted to the thesis's representation, so they have no RGB-D noise. Human3.6M also has only about 7 to 11 subjects, too few for a credible re-ID claim.
- The real-sensor check is one subject now, or 10 to 15 after ethics approval. That is far below the scale of privacy papers, which use thousands of users.
- Utility metrics such as reach, range of motion and handover timing have no clinical ground truth. The thesis's own accuracy is centimetre-level with known failure modes (stale grip offsets, unobservable twist), so the utility tolerance would sit inside the measurement noise. That makes "utility preserved" nearly trivial and unconvincing.
- An adaptive attacker that is aware of canonicalisation, plus linking attacks on residual dynamics, would likely still identify people, as earlier work found for VR (dynamics carry identity beyond height and arm span). The paper would then report a partial defence.
- Edge fps and power are engineering numbers, not contributions at PETS or IEEE VR.

4. Venue fit is poor everywhere:
- PETS or USENIX expect strong threat models and large-scale attacks. The attack here is small and the defence is known.
- IEEE VR, ISMAR and TVCG expect XR telemetry; a third-person RGB-D camera for remote assessment is tangential, and a perceptual study bolted on at the end will not satisfy them.
- TNSRE or JMIR Rehabilitation would care about clinical utility, but there are no patients or clinicians.
The realistic outcome is a workshop paper or a mid-tier venue.

5. The link to the thesis is the weakest of all candidates. Its distinctive assets (object-conditioned recovery, honesty flags, causal merging) are not needed for the privacy contribution. Any skeleton stream with bone lengths would serve.

No clear, defensible, surprising headline claim; incremental method; unconvincing data scale; mismatched venue. Refute.

Prior work found:
- Unique Identification of 50,000+ Virtual Reality Users from Head and Hand Motion Data (USENIX Security 2023); overlap: Shows large-scale identifiability of motion telemetry, which removes the novelty of the attack and identifiability half of the idea.; verified=False
- Deep Motion Masking for Secure, Usable, and Scalable Real-Time Anonymization of Virtual Reality Motion Data (arXiv 2023 (Nair et al.; peer-reviewed venue uncertain)); overlap: Real-time learned anonymisation of motion streams that preserves usability. Same defence template as proposal defence (3).; verified=False
- Anonymization for Skeleton Action Recognition (AAAI 2023 (Moon et al.)); overlap: Adversarial anonymisation of 3D skeleton sequences that keeps action-recognition utility. Closest third-person skeleton analogue of the proposed defence.; verified=False
- Privacy-Preserving Deep Action Recognition: An Adversarial Learning Framework and A New Dataset (IEEE TPAMI 2020 (Wu et al.)); overlap: Template for adversarial privacy-utility trade-off training (on video rather than kinematics).; verified=False
- BOXRR-23: Berkeley Open Extended Reality Recordings 2023 (IEEE VR / TVCG 2024 (approximate)); overlap: Large multi-user motion dataset for XR identification research; sets the expected data scale that this proposal cannot match.; verified=False
- Comparison of data encodings and machine learning architectures for user identification on arbitrary motion sequences (IEEE AIxVR 2022 (Rack et al.)); overlap: Shows that identity lives in dynamics and encoding choice, not just body dimensions. Predicts that canonicalising bone lengths alone will not anonymise.; verified=False

Suggested reframe: If the idea is pursued, change both the claim and the venue rather than polishing the defence.

Option A: a negative, measurement-style claim. "Canonicalising anthropometry is not enough: residual upper-body dynamics in third-person kinematic streams still re-identify people, and the remaining utility-privacy frontier for assessment metrics is X." To support it:
- run a large-scale attack on a public multi-subject third-person dataset (e.g. NTU RGB+D 120 skeletons, about 106 subjects, or AMASS with real-noise augmentation);
- use an adaptive attacker;
- report the full frontier;
- target PETS or ACM CCS/WPES, or the IEEE VR workshop track.

Option B: a clinical-utility claim at TNSRE or JMIR Rehabilitation. This needs real patient or therapist assessment data and clinician-validated metrics.

Either way, drop the edge-hardware and Unity-twin parts, which add nothing reviewers value. Recognise that this direction reuses almost none of the thesis's distinctive contributions; the author would be better served choosing a direction built on object-conditioned recovery or honesty-calibrated tracking.
